* * *

**ChatGPT:**

You can do it close together. I just would not do it as one undifferentiated “big fix everything” task.

The reason is engineering trust.

Why not all at once?

Because Diagnostic Spine and Modularization are different types of work.

Diagnostic Spine = measurement tool
Modularization = surgery


If you do both at the same time and something breaks, you get this mess:

Did the app break because:
[ ] the trace system is wrong?
[ ] the refactor changed behavior?
[ ] imports/module visibility broke?
[ ] payload history rendering is wrong?
[ ] Dev Console is reading wrong state?
[ ] evaluator_form behavior changed?
[ ] old status-updater bug still exists?


That puts us right back into the original problem: unidentifiable failure.

So the cleaner logic is:

1. Prove the measuring tool works.
2. Then use the measuring tool while cutting the code apart.


That is why I said live verify first.

But can we compress it?

Yes. We can compress it into one agent session, but with hard checkpoints.

Not:

Do diagnostic spine and modularization.


Better:

Phase A: verify Diagnostic Spine live.
Commit/checkpoint.
Phase B: modularize evaluator_form only.
Run same tests.
Phase C: run same live smoke and compare traces.


That gets you speed without losing fault isolation.

Current state
[✓] Diagnostic Spine code exists
[✓] Tests pass
[✓] Payload rendering reportedly added
[✓] Dev Console visibility reportedly added
[ ] Live payload confirms trace actually appears
[ ] Modularization done


The risk is this:

If we modularize before verifying trace visibility, and trace visibility fails after refactor,
we will not know whether it was already broken or the refactor broke it.


That is the main reason to pause for one live check.

What I would do
Option A, safest
1. Run one live smoke.
2. Confirm PIPELINE TRACE and EVALUATOR ROW TRACE appear.
3. Then modularize evaluator_form.rs.

No behavior changes.

- Added the finalize_timing method to TurnPipelineTrace in src-tauri/src/pipeline_trace.rs.

# Walkthrough: Modularize evaluator_form.rs

## What Changed

src-tauri/state_engine/src/evaluator_form.rs (originally ~3 600 lines) was split into a directory of focused submodules with no behavior changes.

## Final File Layout


src-tauri/state_engine/src/evaluator_form/
├── mod.rs          93 lines  — facade: sub-mod declarations + 3 pub(crate) helpers
├── types.rs                  — all structs, enums, and basic impls
├── trace.rs                  — trace/status helpers (EvalFormTrace, format_honest_ui_status, …)
├── raw_repair.rs             — raw JSON repair and parse_eval_form_response*
├── normalize.rs              — normalization helpers (normalize_eval_form_response, aliases, …)
├── validate.rs               — per-row validators (validate_event_row, …)
├── compile.rs                — spec builder, compile_eval_form_response, all conversion helpers
└── tests.rs       ~2 700 lines — full unit test suite (file-based #[cfg(test)] mod tests;)


## Key Decisions

- **Public API preserved**: All types and functions that were previously pub remain pub and accessible through state_engine::evaluator_form::* via pub use submod::*; re-exports in mod.rs.
- **Internal helpers**: Functions that only need to be visible within the evaluator_form subtree were made pub(crate). This includes slugify, clean, resolve_active_entity_id (in mod.rs) and various helpers in each submodule.
- **tests.rs visibility**: Because tests.rs is a child module of evaluator_form, use super::* inside it pulls in all the re-exported public and pub(crate) items. No visibility changes were required for the test move.
- **No aliases added**, no behavior changed, no branch/variant logic touched.

## Verification Results

| Check | Result |
|---|---|
| cargo test (state_engine only) | **194 passed, 0 failed** |
| npm run test:rust (full suite incl. db) | **222 passed, 0 failed** |
| npm run typecheck | **Clean** |
| cargo fmt --check | **Clean** |

# Fix: Live Object Row Defaulting

## Problem

validate_object_row rejects rows with "property_changed and new_value are required" or "object_id or new_object_label is required".

Two normalisation gaps in normalize_object_aliases cause this:

1. **Gap 1 — empty new_value when property_changed = "state"**: The function only fills new_value from evidence_quote when change_type == "state_change". If the LLM already set property_changed = "state" but left new_value blank (e.g. via the object_state alias), the fallback never fires.

2. **Gap 2 — missing object_id and new_object_label**: When neither field is present and change_type is not set, no object ID can be inferred. The fix adds a conservative noun-extraction pass against evidence_quote using a static list of common physical nouns (jacket, chair, door, table, …). Abstract terms are rejected.

## Proposed Changes

### normalize.rs — normalize_object_aliases

**Gap 1 fix** (lines ~1387-1391): After the change_type-based new_value block, add a second fallback:


rust
// Gap 1: property_changed already "state" but new_value still empty
if row.new_value.trim().is_empty() && row.property_changed == "state" {
    if !row.evidence_quote.trim().is_empty() {
        row.new_value = row.evidence_quote.clone();
    }
}


**Gap 2 fix** (lines ~1372-1376): After the new_object_label → object_id slug block, add:


rust
// Gap 2: infer conservative object label from evidence when id still missing
if row.object_id.as_deref().and_then(clean).is_none() {
    if let Some(label) = infer_physical_object_from_evidence(&row.evidence_quote) {
        row.new_object_label = Some(label.to_string());
        row.object_id = Some(slugify(label));
    }
}


Add new pub(crate) fn infer_physical_object_from_evidence(evidence: &str) -> Option<&'static str> that checks for a static list of known physical nouns in the evidence string. Returns None for abstract text (no match).

### validate.rs

No changes needed. After normalization fills the gaps, the existing validator passes.

### compile.rs

No changes needed. The object compilation path already handles inferred IDs (line 253-262).

### tests.rs

Five new tests:
- object_row_empty_new_value_derives_from_evidence_quote
- object_row_missing_id_infers_wet_jacket_from_evidence
- object_row_missing_id_infers_chair_from_evidence
- object_row_without_physical_object_still_rejects
- live_wet_jacket_chair_rows_create_object_patch

## Verification Plan


cargo test --manifest-path src-tauri/state_engine/Cargo.toml
npm.cmd run test:rust
npm.cmd run typecheck
cargo fmt --manifest-path src-tauri/Cargo.toml --check

# Implementation Plan: Strip Leaked Assistant Closing Tags From Narrator Output 

## Goal
To prevent </assistant> tags from leaking into any saved or displayed narrator responses. Trailing instances of </assistant> (including repeated trailing instances) must be stripped from the visible response.

## Proposed Changes

We will implement a robust tag-stripping utility in the state_engine::hidden_state module and apply it in both response parsing and the output contract guard.

### 1. [hidden_state.rs](file:///c:/Users/T-ROBOTICS/Downloads/mnemosyne/src-tauri/state_engine/src/hidden_state.rs)

#### [MODIFY] strip_assistant_close_tag and parse_hidden_state
- Add a new public function strip_assistant_close_tag(s: &str) -> String that repeatedly strips trailing </assistant> tags from a trimmed string:
  
rust
  pub fn strip_assistant_close_tag(s: &str) -> String {
      let tag = "</assistant>";
      let mut current = s.to_string();
      loop {
          let trimmed = current.trim_end();
          if let Some(stripped) = trimmed.strip_suffix(tag) {
              current = stripped.to_string();
          } else {
              break;
          }
      }
      current.trim_end().to_string()
  }

- Use strip_assistant_close_tag inside parse_hidden_state to clean visible_text right as it is separated from any hidden state.

---

### 2. [commands.rs](file:///c:/Users/T-ROBOTICS/Downloads/mnemosyne/src-tauri/src/commands.rs)

#### [MODIFY] apply_output_contract_guard_core
- Call state_engine::hidden_state::strip_assistant_close_tag(content) at the very beginning of the function so all downstream checks (hidden state block check, engine patch check, status block parsing, pure OOC check) operate on a clean body.

#### [NEW TESTS]
We will add the following four unit tests to the tests module in commands.rs to verify the functionality:
- narrator_visible_response_strips_trailing_assistant_close_tag
- ooc_response_strips_trailing_assistant_close_tag
- status_block_response_strips_assistant_tag_but_preserves_status
- evaluator_json_with_outer_assistant_tag_still_parses

---

# Implementation Plan: Strip Leaked Assistant Closing Tags (With User Additions)

## Goal
To prevent </assistant> tags from leaking into saved or displayed responses, especially when repeated at the end, or when they appear between the body prose and the status block during normalization.

## Proposed Changes

We will implement a robust tag-stripping utility in state_engine::hidden_state and apply it at multiple stages of response processing.

### 1. [hidden_state.rs](file:///c:/Users/T-ROBOTICS/Downloads/mnemosyne/src-tauri/state_engine/src/hidden_state.rs)

#### [MODIFY] strip_assistant_close_tag and parse_hidden_state
- Implement strip_assistant_close_tag(s: &str) -> String to repeatedly strip trailing </assistant> tags:
  
rust
  pub fn strip_assistant_close_tag(s: &str) -> String {
      let tag = "</assistant>";
      let mut current = s.to_string();
      loop {
          let trimmed = current.trim_end();
          if let Some(stripped) = trimmed.strip_suffix(tag) {
              current = stripped.to_string();
          } else {
              break;
          }
      }
      current.trim_end().to_string()
  }

- Clean visible_text inside parse_hidden_state with strip_assistant_close_tag.

---

### 2. [commands.rs](file:///c:/Users/T-ROBOTICS/Downloads/mnemosyne/src-tauri/src/commands.rs)

#### [MODIFY] apply_output_contract_guard_core
We will apply strip_assistant_close_tag at three crucial checkpoints to ensure no tags escape:
1. **Entry**: Strip at entry from content.
2. **Body Prose**: Strip from the extracted body before appending the status block to prevent any tags from being sandwiched between body and status.
3. **Final Return**: Strip at final-return from the constructed normalized output.

#### [NEW TESTS]
Add the following tests to the tests module in commands.rs:
- narrator_visible_response_strips_trailing_assistant_close_tag: Basic stripping.
- ooc_response_strips_trailing_assistant_close_tag: Meta Turn / OOC stripping.
- status_block_response_strips_assistant_tag_but_preserves_status: Strips tag at the very end of status.
- evaluator_json_with_outer_assistant_tag_still_parses: Verifies parser logic ignores outer tags.
- repeated_trailing_tags_are_stripped: Verifies that multiple sequential tags (e.g. </assistant></assistant>) are completely removed.
- status_block_repair_strips_assistant_tag_between_body_and_status: Verifies that tags between the body and status blocks are successfully stripped and do not end up sandwiched between them.

**You:**

question: narrator elapsed time is for the timeline of the fictional story no? or do i know it wrong?

* * *

**ChatGPT:**

No, you had it slightly wrong.

There are two different “time” concepts in Mnemosyne:

1. Fictional/story time
2. Backend/runtime elapsed time

1. Fictional/story time

This is the in-world timeline. In your prompt/context it appears as:

Time elapsed: Late evening, just after midnight.


That belongs to the WORLD SNAPSHOT and describes the scene’s fictional time. 

mnemosyne-local-mock-21728a23-d…

Examples:

Late evening
just after midnight
two days later
morning
after the party


This should affect narration and continuity.

2. Backend/runtime elapsed time

This is the real software timing. In the pipeline trace:

total_elapsed_ms: 2993
narrator_called: 2879ms
context_compiled: 11ms
assistant_saved: 45ms


That means the app spent about 2.879 real seconds waiting for the narrator model call, not that 2.879 seconds passed inside the story. 

mnemosyne-local-mock-21728a23-d…

So:

narrator_called elapsed_ms = API/model latency
evaluator_response_received elapsed_ms = evaluator model latency
context_compiled elapsed_ms = backend context build time
session_state_rebuilt elapsed_ms = DB/state rebuild time
total_elapsed_ms = real pipeline duration


The roadmap also defines these as performance metrics: context_compile_ms, narrator_request_ms, evaluator_provider_ms, patch_compile_ms, materialized_rebuild_ms, and total_turn_ms. These are explicitly for diagnosing slowness, not fictional timeline. 

road-map-ver-2

Why Payload 3 is weird

This part:

total_elapsed_ms: 0
- narrator_called: 10680ms
- evaluator_response_received: 16719ms


is not story time. It is a diagnostics/timing bug or stale pre-finalization trace. It is mathematically inconsistent because stage elapsed times are nonzero while total is zero. 

mnemosyne-local-mock-21728a23-d…

So the correct interpretation is:

Payload 3 total_elapsed_ms = 0
→ trace finalization issue

Payload 4 total_elapsed_ms = 27533
→ actual backend runtime for the completed narrator + evaluator path

Clean terminology I’d use

To avoid confusion, rename or label them like this:

story_time_elapsed = fictional continuity time
pipeline_total_elapsed_ms = backend wall-clock runtime
narrator_request_ms = narrator API/model latency
evaluator_provider_ms = evaluator API/model latency


Bottom line:

Narrator elapsed time is NOT fictional story time.
It is real backend/model execution time.


The fictional timeline is the Time elapsed: field inside WORLD SNAPSHOT.

state_engine/src/evaluator_form.rs

Malformed JSON?       raw_repair.rs
Alias/default issue?  normalize.rs
Rejected row?         validate.rs
Patch conversion?     compile.rs
Trace/debug output?   trace.rs
Struct/schema issue?  types.rs
Regression test?      tests.rs


That is real modularization.

PipelineErrorCode

validate_object_row()

call shared helper/function


instead of:

copy similar logic everywhere manually


The visible </assistant> cleanup is a good example. Instead of ad-hoc stripping in UI/export/chat, it now uses a central state_engine::hidden_state::strip_assistant_close_tag and applies it at parsing/guard boundaries.

The whole codebase is not clean yet.

Known remaining large areas:

# Implementation Plan: Strip Leaked Assistant Closing Tags (With User Additions)

## Goal
To prevent </assistant> tags from leaking into saved or displayed responses, especially when repeated at the end, or when they appear between the body prose and the status block during normalization.

## Proposed Changes

We will implement a robust tag-stripping utility in state_engine::hidden_state and apply it at multiple stages of response processing.

### 1. [hidden_state.rs](file:///c:/Users/T-ROBOTICS/Downloads/mnemosyne/src-tauri/state_engine/src/hidden_state.rs)

#### [MODIFY] strip_assistant_close_tag and parse_hidden_state
- Implement strip_assistant_close_tag(s: &str) -> String to repeatedly strip trailing </assistant> tags:
  
rust
  pub fn strip_assistant_close_tag(s: &str) -> String {
      let tag = "</assistant>";
      let mut current = s.to_string();
      loop {
          let trimmed = current.trim_end();
          if let Some(stripped) = trimmed.strip_suffix(tag) {
              current = stripped.to_string();
          } else {
              break;
          }
      }
      current.trim_end().to_string()
  }

- Clean visible_text inside parse_hidden_state with strip_assistant_close_tag.

---

### 2. [commands.rs](file:///c:/Users/T-ROBOTICS/Downloads/mnemosyne/src-tauri/src/commands.rs)

#### [MODIFY] apply_output_contract_guard_core
We will apply strip_assistant_close_tag at three crucial checkpoints to ensure no tags escape:
1. **Entry**: Strip at entry from content.
2. **Body Prose**: Strip from the extracted body before appending the status block to prevent any tags from being sandwiched between body and status.
3. **Final Return**: Strip at final-return from the constructed normalized output.

#### [NEW TESTS]
Add the following tests to the tests module in commands.rs:
- narrator_visible_response_strips_trailing_assistant_close_tag: Basic stripping.
- ooc_response_strips_trailing_assistant_close_tag: Meta Turn / OOC stripping.
- status_block_response_strips_assistant_tag_but_preserves_status: Strips tag at the very end of status.
- evaluator_json_with_outer_assistant_tag_still_parses: Verifies parser logic ignores outer tags.
- repeated_trailing_tags_are_stripped: Verifies that multiple sequential tags (e.g. </assistant></assistant>) are completely removed.
- status_block_repair_strips_assistant_tag_between_body_and_status: Verifies that tags between the body and status blocks are successfully stripped and do not end up sandwiched between them.

---

## Verification Plan

   npm.cmd run test:rust

2. Verify typescript typechecking:
   
npm.cmd run typecheck

3. Check code formatting:
   
cargo fmt --manifest-path src-tauri/Cargo.toml --check

# Implementation Plan: P0.1B Data Safety, Soul / Savepoint Archive + Restore

Implement safe soft-archiving and restoration of Souls and Savepoints (both embedded in the same souls database table) to completely prevent unrecoverable data loss in normal UI paths.

## Phase A: Audit Soul/savepoint destructive paths

### Audit Summary Table

| Function / Action | File Path | Hard-Deletes Rows? | Exposed to UI? | Recommended Safe Replacement |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| delete_soul | src-tauri/src/db/mod.rs (line 1228) | Yes, executes DELETE FROM souls WHERE character_id = ?1 | Yes, exposed via Tauri command delete_soul to React deleteSoul() | Change delete_soul to call archive_soul (or map to a soft-archiving helper) to set archived_at = Some(timestamp) rather than hard-deleting the row. |
| Custom Character / Savepoint Deletions | Embedded in souls table | Yes, because custom savepoints are stored in the souls table (with soul_kind = 'savepoint' or 'checkpoint'). | Yes, managed via delete_soul | Soft-archive savepoints by introducing archive_savepoint and archive_soul which write archived_at in the souls table and filter active lists. |

### Cascading Deletions Risk Analysis
If a Soul row is hard-deleted from souls, it triggers SQL-level foreign key cascades (ON DELETE CASCADE):
1. **conversations** matching the Soul's soul_id are hard-deleted.
2. **messages** inside those conversations are hard-deleted.
3. **turn_snapshots**, **assistant_message_variants**, and **state_patches** associated with those conversations/messages are cascade-deleted.
Soft-archiving entirely prevents this cascade of data destruction.

---

## Phase B: Schema Migration

We will perform a single, safe migration adding a nullable archived_at column to the souls table. Since "Souls" and "Savepoints" are both stored inside the souls table (differentiated by soul_kind), this single column covers both cleanly:

### Migration Statement

rust
add_column_if_missing(conn, "souls", "archived_at", "INTEGER")?;


---

## Phase C: Soul Archive / Restore

### Database Functions
1. **archive_soul(conn, id)**:
   Sets archived_at = Some(now_ts()) for the given Soul ID. Does not touch or cascade-delete conversations, savepoints, worlds, messages, payload logs, state patches, or memories.
2. **restore_soul(conn, id)**:
   Clears archived_at = None for the given Soul ID, restoring it to the active list.
3. **list_archived_souls(conn)**:
   Lists all Souls with archived_at IS NOT NULL (and soul_kind != 'session_clone').
4. **Update list_souls(conn)**:
   Add AND archived_at IS NULL to the filter.
5. **Update list_souls_including_session_clones(conn)**:
   Add WHERE archived_at IS NULL (or filter appropriately).

---

## Phase D: Savepoint Archive / Restore

Since savepoints are also stored in the souls table (as soul_kind = 'savepoint' or 'checkpoint'), we will implement:
1. **archive_savepoint(conn, id)**:
   Sets archived_at = Some(now_ts()) on the savepoint Soul row.
2. **restore_savepoint(conn, id)**:
   Sets archived_at = None on the savepoint Soul row.
3. **list_archived_savepoints(conn)**:
   Lists all savepoint Souls (or checkpoint Souls) where archived_at IS NOT NULL.

---

## Phase E: UI Wording & Tauri Commands

1. **Commands to expose**:
   - archive_soul
   - restore_soul
   - list_archived_souls
   - archive_savepoint
   - restore_savepoint
   - list_archived_savepoints
2. **UI Wording updates**:
   - "Delete selected Soul" -> "Archive selected Soul"
   - Confirm prompt: "Delete Aurora Schwarz and all local chats... This cannot be undone." -> "Archive Aurora Schwarz? Local chats and savepoint history remain safe and recoverable."
   - Map frontend deleteSoul to invoke archive_soul (or soft-archive) in Tauri.

---

## Phase F: Verification Plan

### Automated Tests
We will add the following tests to src-tauri/src/db/mod.rs to verify all archive, restore, and cascade-free properties:
- archive_soul_hides_from_active_soul_list
- restore_soul_reappears_in_active_soul_list
- archive_soul_does_not_delete_conversations
- archive_soul_does_not_delete_messages
- archive_soul_does_not_delete_savepoints
- archive_soul_does_not_delete_payload_logs
- archive_one_soul_does_not_affect_sibling_souls
- archive_savepoint_hides_from_active_list
- restore_savepoint_reappears_in_active_list
- archive_savepoint_does_not_delete_checkpoint_data
- archive_savepoint_does_not_affect_sibling_savepoints
- restore_savepoint_preserves_linked_soul_and_world

We will run:

powershell
npm.cmd run test:rust
npm.cmd run typecheck
cargo fmt --manifest-path src-tauri/Cargo.toml --check

# Walkthrough: P0.2A API Provider Profile Safety (Archive / Restore / Active Protection)

We successfully implemented complete provider/API profile soft-archiving, restoration, and active profile protection. Users can no longer hard-delete their API profiles from the normal UI. Active narrator and updater profiles are fully protected against accidental archiving.

---

## 1. Summary of Changes Made

### Schema & Database Migration
- Added nullable archived_at INTEGER column to the provider_profiles table.
- Added archived_at field to ProviderProfile type definitions in both Rust (db/mod.rs) and TypeScript (tauri.ts).
- Created safe, automated migration in run_migrations.

### Backend Implementation (src-tauri/src/db/mod.rs)
- **archive_provider_profile(conn, id, active_ids)**: Soft-archives the profile (sets archived_at = Some(now)). Prevents archiving the profile if its ID is present in active_ids (the currently selected narrator/updater profiles), returning a detailed validation error: "Cannot archive the active provider profile. Switch profiles first."
- **restore_provider_profile(conn, id)**: Restores the profile (sets archived_at = NULL).
- **list_archived_provider_profiles(conn)**: Lists all archived profiles sorted by when they were archived.
- **list_provider_profiles(conn)**: Filtered to return only active, non-archived profiles (WHERE archived_at IS NULL).
- **Fail-safe delete_provider_profile**: Mapped backend delete_provider_profile to use soft-archiving instead of database row deletion, guaranteeing that any legacy calls never hard-delete.

### Tauri Command Layer (src-tauri/src/commands.rs, src-tauri/src/lib.rs)
- Registered and exposed archive_provider_profile, restore_provider_profile, and list_archived_provider_profiles Tauri commands.

### Frontend UI Integration (src/App.tsx, src/tauri.ts)
- Mapped Tauri commands in src/tauri.ts to archiveProviderProfile, restoreProviderProfile, and listArchivedProviderProfiles.
- Updated React handlers in src/App.tsx:
  - handleArchiveProviderProfile: Constructs active profile ID arrays, checks if the target profile is currently active in narrator/updater slots, blocks and alerts the user with "Cannot archive the active provider profile. Switch profiles first.", and executes the soft-archiving call.
  - handleRestoreProviderProfile: Restores any archived profile back to active duty.
- Re-labeled all card buttons from "Delete" to "Archive Profile" with Archive icon instead of trash cans.

# Harden Legacy delete_provider_profile Safety

## Context & Goal
In P0.2A, provider profile archiving/restoring and frontend active-profile guards were introduced. However, the legacy delete_provider_profile backend Tauri command redirects to archive_provider_profile with active_ids = []. If a client calls this backend command directly, it can bypass the active-profile archive protection (i.e. archive a currently active provider profile).

The goal is to harden this legacy backend safety so that delete_provider_profile cannot be used as an active-profile archive bypass.

We choose **Option A** (safest), which is to make delete_provider_profile return a deprecated error. This option is highly preferred since the UI exclusively uses archive_provider_profile and does not require compatibility with delete_provider_profile.

Additionally, we ensure that **no hard deletion** happens anywhere, changing any database-level deletion to a safe archiving process under the hood.

## Proposed Changes

### Tauri Backend Commands

#### [MODIFY] [commands.rs](file:///c:/Users/T-ROBOTICS/Downloads/mnemosyne/src-tauri/src/commands.rs)
- Modify delete_provider_profile to immediately return an error:
  Err("delete_provider_profile is deprecated; use archive_provider_profile with active profile guard.".into())

### Database Engine

#### [MODIFY] [mod.rs](file:///c:/Users/T-ROBOTICS/Downloads/mnemosyne/src-tauri/src/db/mod.rs)
- Modify db::delete_provider_profile so that it does **not** hard delete (DELETE FROM provider_profiles). Instead, it should perform an archive (UPDATE provider_profiles SET archived_at = ?). This fulfills the "no hard delete" rule at all levels.
- Add unit tests verifying the requested behavior:
  - delete_provider_profile_no_longer_hard_deletes
  - delete_provider_profile_cannot_archive_active_profile_or_returns_deprecated_error
  - archive_provider_profile_still_blocks_active_profile
  - archive_provider_profile_still_archives_inactive_profile

## Verification Plan

### Automated Tests
1. Run Rust unit tests to verify database and command-level safety:
   npm.cmd run test:rust or cargo test
2. Run Tauri build checks:
   cargo check --manifest-path src-tauri/Cargo.toml
3. Run cargo fmt check:
   cargo fmt --manifest-path src-tauri/Cargo.toml --check
4. Run frontend type checking:
   npm.cmd run typecheck

delete_provider_profile → error

Legacy DB function:
delete_provider_profile → error/no-op

Hard delete:
not available from normal UI

# Walkthrough - P0.2B Settings Safety, Archive / Restore / Hard-Delete Guard

We successfully designed, implemented, and validated the settings safety archiving and restoring model to protect user-created settings/world/location configurations from hard deletes.

## Changes Made

### 1. Database Schema & Migration
- **Added** settings.archived_at as the source of truth:
  - Updated CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS settings statement in src-tauri/src/db/mod.rs to include archived_at INTEGER (nullable).
  - Added migration instruction using add_column_if_missing to safely add archived_at to existing settings tables without losing any data.

### 2. Database Operations (db/mod.rs)
- **Updated** SettingSummary struct to include pub archived_at: Option<i64>.
- **Modified** list_settings to select archived_at and only return active settings (WHERE archived_at IS NULL).
- **Implemented** settings archive and restore engine:
  - archive_setting(conn, setting_id, active_or_default_ids):
    - Blocks archiving if the setting ID is in the active_or_default_ids list.
    - Blocks archiving if this setting is the last remaining non-archived setting in the database (returning "Cannot archive the active/default setting. Switch settings first.").
    - Sets archived_at = Some(now_ts()) upon success, preserving all configuration fields intact.
  - restore_setting(conn, setting_id): Clears archived_at (sets to NULL).
  - list_archived_settings(conn): Returns only archived settings (WHERE archived_at IS NOT NULL).
- **Deprecated** delete_setting to return a ToSqlConversionFailure error.
- **Added** delete_setting_internal (unreachable from Tauri/frontend, decorated with #[allow(dead_code)]) strictly for unit test cleanups.

### 3. Tauri Command Layer (commands.rs & lib.rs)
- **Exposed** new commands: archive_setting, restore_setting, and list_archived_settings.
- **Modified** delete_setting command to return the deprecated error: "delete_setting is deprecated; use archive_setting with active/default setting guard.".
- **Registered** all new commands in src-tauri/src/lib.rs.

### 4. Frontend Wiring (src/tauri.ts & src/App.tsx)
- **Updated** SettingSummary type to include optional archived_at.

Task: P0.3A Import / Export Roundtrip Hardening

Normal UI data-loss paths are now mostly protected. The next alpha-critical target is making sure exported .mne bundles can be validated, previewed, and restored/imported safely without corrupting existing local data.

Goal:
Harden .mne export/import roundtrip reliability. Exported bundles should be structurally valid, importable into a clean database, and safe to preview before restore. Import must never overwrite existing user data silently.

Hard rules:

No destructive overwrite on import.
Do not touch evaluator_form.
Do not touch narrator/evaluator prompts.
Do not touch relationship semantics.
Do not touch branch/variant generation.
Do not change archive/restore behavior unless a compile fix requires it.
Do not build a full polished import UI unless the backend/import logic is already solid.
Import should default to non-destructive “restore as new copy” behavior.
If overwrite/merge is not fully safe, do not implement it yet.

Recommended hardening action

if branch/variant import is not yet safe, explicitly skip with warning rather than corrupting data

Existing local IDs must not be overwritten.
Imported IDs can be remapped.
Store old_id -> new_id map in import report.

Task: Relationship Semantics Polish, Direction Inference for Strong Evidence

Context:
P0 data safety and import/export hardening are mostly complete. The current live smoke shows .mne export works, visible chat is clean, memory/object/scene state export works, but relationship rows still fail when the evaluator omits direction.

Live failure:
Evaluator row trace showed:

row_kind: relationship
dimension: boundary_pressure
evidence_quote: "The chain is still on the door—she hasn't decided if she's expecting someone or preparing for a stranger—but she's already reaching for the key."
direction: null
validation_status: rejected
rejection_reason: direction_missing_uncertain
compiler_result: rejected

Goal:
Make relationship rows with strong directional evidence compile when the evaluator omits direction, without over-inferencing weak or ambiguous rows.

Hard rules:

Do not touch data safety/archive/restore/import/export code.
Do not touch branch/variant logic.
Do not touch narrator prompts.
Do not change memory/object/event behavior.
Do not weaken validation globally.
Do not accept every missing-direction relationship row.
Keep this inside evaluator_form modules only unless tests require imports.

Target files:

src-tauri/state_engine/src/evaluator_form/normalize.rs
src-tauri/state_engine/src/evaluator_form/validate.rs
src-tauri/state_engine/src/evaluator_form/compile.rs
src-tauri/state_engine/src/evaluator_form/tests.rs
src-tauri/state_engine/src/evaluator_form/trace.rs only if trace labeling needs improvement

Required behavior:

For boundary_pressure, infer direction = increase when evidence strongly implies guardedness, boundary, uncertainty, or protected access.
Examples:
"chain is still on the door"
"hasn't decided if she's expecting someone or preparing for a stranger"
"keeps the door chained"
"hesitates before opening"
"holds the door partly closed"
"keeps distance"
"backs away"
"sets a boundary"
"refuses entry"
For trust, infer direction = decrease only when evidence strongly implies distrust, suspicion, refusal, guardedness, or verification behavior.
Examples:
"doesn't trust"
"backs away suspiciously"
"keeps the chain on"
"asks who sent you"
"refuses to open"
For comfort, infer:
decrease when evidence implies tension, discomfort, guardedness, or unease.
increase when evidence implies relaxing, softening, inviting closer, opening the door fully, or comfortable familiarity.
For fear, infer increase when evidence implies fear/anxiety/startle/hypervigilance.
Examples:
"stiffens"
"pulse thrumming"
"taste copper"
"startled"
"flinches"
"fear spikes"
Do not infer relationship direction from generic body language alone unless tied to relationship-relevant context.
Reject or keep uncertain:
"she looks at him"
"she watches"
"she listens"
"she smiles"
"she pauses"
"she breathes"
Row trace should clearly show when direction was inferred:
relationship_direction_inferred_from
normalized row has direction
compiler_result should become relationship_delta_created or equivalent
If evidence remains ambiguous, keep current behavior:
rejected
direction_missing_uncertain

Implementation guidance:

Prefer implementing this in normalization, not validation.
Add a helper like:
infer_relationship_direction_from_evidence(dimension, evidence_quote, shift, summary) -> Option<RelationshipDirection>
Use dimension-specific phrase tables.
Keep the phrase table conservative.
Add trace warnings/decisions so the Dev Console explains the inference.

Tests:

boundary_pressure_infers_increase_from_door_chain
boundary_pressure_infers_increase_from_preparing_for_stranger
trust_infers_decrease_from_guarded_door_chain
comfort_infers_decrease_from_guarded_uncertain_entry
fear_infers_increase_from_stiffens_and_pulse
generic_watching_does_not_infer_direction
ambiguous_relationship_row_still_rejects_direction_missing_uncertain
live_knock_boundary_pressure_row_compiles
row_trace_records_relationship_direction_inference

**You:**

# Plan - P0.3A Import / Export Roundtrip Hardening

Harden .mne export/import roundtrip reliability. Exported bundles should be structurally valid, importable into a clean database, and safe to preview before restore. Import must never overwrite existing user data silently.

## Safety Constraints

* **No destructive overwrite on import.**
* Do not touch evaluator_form.
* Do not touch narrator/evaluator prompts.
* Do not touch relationship semantics.
* Do not touch branch/variant generation.
* Import should default to non-destructive “restore as new copy” behavior.

---

## 1. Export/Import Paths Audit

| Function Name | File Path | What it Exports/Imports | Whether Import Exists? | Overwrites Existing Data? | Payload Logs Included? | Patches / Commits / Variants Included? | Object States, Events, Scene, Memory, Relationships Survive? | Recommended Hardening Action |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| export_current_session_checkpoint_mne | src-tauri/src/commands.rs | Current soul, session world, conversation, messages, payload logs | Yes | No (Tauri layers only expose non-destructive copy) | Yes, dynamically | No, packages active branch only | Yes | Hardy validate bundle and preserve all states on import |
| import_mne_as_new | src-tauri/src/commands.rs | Entire .mne package into DB | Yes | No (always remaps colliding IDs to new UUIDs) | Yes | Yes (active path) | Yes, fully | Map missing/colliding IDs and verify foreign key safety |
| preview_mne_import | src-tauri/src/commands.rs | Manifest & counts summary | Yes (dry run) | No (readonly) | Yes | N/A | Yes | Dry-run parsing without any SQLite side-effects |
| validate_mne_bundle | src-tauri/src/commands.rs | Bundle structure & schemas | Yes (validation) | No (readonly) | Yes | N/A | Yes | Strict zip, schema, ID match, and file check |

---

## 2. Decided Safety & Collision/Remap Model

1. **Non-Destructive Remapping**:
   - When importing, import_mne_as_new checks for duplicate IDs using db::get_soul, db::get_session_world, db::get_setting, and db::get_conversation_summary.
   - If any ID exists locally, a new unique UUID V4 is generated and stored in a remapping map old_id -> new_id.
   - All references (e.g., soul_id in conversation, world_id in conversation, owner_soul_id in memories) are safely updated to their remapped counterparts before database insertion.

2. **Foreign Key Integrity**:
   - session_worlds and conversations have strict SQLite foreign key constraints referencing settings(setting_id).
   - To prevent constraint failures on clean DB imports, if source_setting_id is set on the imported SessionWorld or Conversation but the setting does not exist locally, it is safely set to None since the field is nullable (ON DELETE SET NULL).

---

## 3. Proposed Changes

### Tauri Backend & Commands (commands.rs & lib.rs)
- **Implement** validate_mne_bundle_bytes, validate_mne_bundle, preview_mne_import, and import_mne_as_new_inner / import_mne_as_new.
- **Implement** schema and ID validation verifying zip format, manifest presence, ID consistency across soul, world, conversation, messages, and payload logs.
- **Implement** structured warnings for unrecognized extra files and clear structured errors for missing required files or invalid JSON format.
- **Register** all commands in lib.rs and verify typecheck passes.

### Frontend Dev UI (App.tsx & tauri.ts)
- **Implement** TypeScript interfaces: MneValidationSummary, MneValidationReport, MneImportResult.
- **Expose** front-end wrappers for validateMneBundle, previewMneImport, and importMneAsNew.
- **Introduce** minimal UI buttons in the Dev Console Actions panel for developers to test and verify bundle files directly.

---

        "evidence_quote": "It's not the confident drawl of a stranger, nor the clipped wariness of someone demanding entry—it's... softer. Familiar in a way that makes her chest tighten just a fraction.",
        "tags": ["relationship"]
      }
    ],
    "memory_rows": [...]
  }
}


That is the important part. The relationship rows existed. They were missing direction. That is exactly the live shape we wanted to test. 

mnemosyne-local-mock-8715090c-7…

The actual bug

The parser expected something closer to:

{
  "event_rows": [],
  "object_rows": [],
  "relationship_rows": [],
  "memory_rows": [],
  "review_rows": []
}


But the model wrapped the form inside:

{
  "evaluator_form_v1": {
    ...
  }
}


The result:

form_response_parse_status: partial_success
form_rows_submitted: 0
form_rows_accepted: 0
form_rows_rejected: 0
draft_relationship_delta_count: 0
relationship_evaluations: []
memory_candidates: []


The payload history confirms that the parse path ended with zero submitted rows and zero accepted rows despite valid rows existing inside the wrapper. 

mnemosyne-local-mock-8715090c-7…

Translation

The missing-direction inference code did not fail.

It never got the row.

raw evaluator output had relationship rows
→ parser failed to unwrap evaluator_form_v1
→ no rows reached normalizer
→ no direction inference ran
→ no relationship delta


So the next fix is not more relationship semantics. It is form envelope unwrapping.

P2 quality:
- prose forgets tone

**ChatGPT:**

Yes. That idea is not dumb. It is actually close to the architecture I would want eventually, but with one important correction:

Do not let the LLM freely rewrite Soul files directly.

Let the LLM propose structured patches.
Let code validate and apply those patches into segmented files / DB / indexes.


The real design is not “one giant Soul markdown file.” It is a Soul Vault.

Core idea

Instead of one monolithic Soul blob:

Aurora_Schwarz.soul.md


Use a folder:

souls/
└── aurora_schwarz/
    ├── manifest.json
    ├── core.md
    ├── status.md
    ├── relationships/
    │   ├── default_player.md
    │   └── other_character.md
    ├── locations/
    │   ├── apartment.md
    │   └── hallway.md
    ├── objects/
    │   ├── phone.md
    │   ├── door_chain.md
    │   └── wet_jacket.md
    ├── plots/
    │   ├── current.md
    │   └── resolved.md
    ├── memories/
    │   ├── relationship_memory.md
    │   ├── unresolved_tension.md
    │   ├── recent_emotional_state.md
    │   └── world_location_memory.md
    └── provenance/
        └── memory_sources.jsonl


Then the context compiler does not load everything. It selects only relevant files.

Example:

Current scene: Aurora apartment doorway
Active entity: default_player
Active objects: door_chain, phone
Active tension: trust / boundary pressure

Load:
- core.md, compressed
- status.md
- relationships/default_player.md
- locations/apartment.md
- objects/door_chain.md
- memories/unresolved_tension.md, top 3 only
- memories/recent_emotional_state.md, top 2 only


That is how you cut token waste.

Why this is powerful

Right now the system is basically:

DB state + materialized summaries + prompt compiler


Your idea adds:

human-readable memory filesystem


That helps because agents and users can inspect/edit it naturally.

Benefits
[✓] Easier for agents to inspect
[✓] Easier for humans to understand
[✓] Easier to diff in Git
[✓] Easier to export/import
[✓] Easier to target relevant context
[✓] Easier to debug memory corruption
[✓] Less need to read giant payload histories


Instead of asking:

Why did Aurora remember this?


You can inspect:

relationships/default_player.md
memories/unresolved_tension.md
provenance/memory_sources.jsonl


That is much better.

But here is the danger

Markdown is not a safe database by itself.

If agents constantly rewrite markdown files, you get:

[!] lost updates
[!] duplicated memories
[!] broken formatting
[!] no transactions
[!] hard-to-detect corruption
[!] merge conflicts
[!] stale indexes
[!] impossible rollback unless backed by patch ledger


So the correct architecture is not:

LLM directly edits core.md


It should be:

LLM/evaluator emits structured patch
→ code validates patch
→ code updates DB / ledger
→ code materializes readable .md files
→ retrieval engine indexes those files


Or, if markdown becomes canonical:

LLM proposes diff
→ code validates diff against schema
→ code applies atomically
→ DB index updates
→ backup snapshot created


The key is tool-gated writes, not freeform file rewriting.

Best design: hybrid source of truth

I would recommend this:

Canonical truth:
SQLite DB + patch ledger

Human/agent-readable projection:
Soul Vault markdown files

Search/retrieval:
SQLite index + embeddings + file metadata


So the DB remains the reliable transactional source, but the markdown vault becomes the readable/editable surface.

That gives you both:

machine safety
+
agent readability

Example file roles
core.md

Slow-changing identity.

# Core Identity

Name: Aurora Schwarz
Role: freelance illustrator
Durable traits:
- sarcastic under pressure
- warm when comfortable
- values control over her own space
- secretly craves genuine connection

Hard boundaries:
- ...


This should change rarely.

Update difficulty: high
Loaded often: yes, compressed

status.md

Fast-changing current state.

# Current Status

Scene: apartment doorway
Emotion: cautious but softening
Body state: tense, tired, rain-night alertness
Immediate focus: deciding whether to open the door further
Current pressure point: door chain still separates her from the visitor
Equipment/clothing: oversized button-up, leggings, barefoot


This changes often.

Update difficulty: low
Loaded every turn

relationships/default_player.md

Relationship state with one character.

# Relationship: Aurora Schwarz -> default_player

Metrics:
- trust: 1
- comfort: 11
- boundary_pressure: 1
- curiosity: 10
- fear: 20

Current dynamic:
Aurora is cautious but slightly reassured by the visitor staying outside the threshold and not forcing entry.

Important moments:
- The visitor knocked silently while Aurora kept the chain engaged.
- The visitor kept hands visible and said she did not have to open the door.


Loaded only when default_player is relevant.

locations/apartment.md

Location memory.

# Location: Aurora's Apartment

Current state:
- rain streaks the windows
- neon light outside
- door chain engaged
- living room low-lit
- phone notifications off

Persistent objects:
- wine glass
- couch
- sketchbook
- phone
- door chain


Loaded only when scene is there.

objects/door_chain.md

Object continuity.

# Object: Door Chain

Status:
Still engaged. Door can open only a few centimeters.

Narrative meaning:
Boundary marker between Aurora's safe interior space and the unknown visitor outside.

Last changed:
Turn 2, Aurora opened the deadbolt slightly but kept the chain on.


This is huge for continuity.

Token-saving model

The important optimization is not “markdown files” by themselves. It is:

file segmentation + retrieval ranking + small selected excerpts


Context compiler should decide:

Always include:
- status.md
- compressed core.md

Conditionally include:
- relationship file for active target
- current location file
- active object files
- top relevant memories by slot
- current plot file

Do not include:
- old resolved plots
- unrelated relationships
- unrelated locations
- full memory archive


That gives you a context like:

Core Identity: 300 tokens
Current Status: 250 tokens
Relationship/default_player: 300 tokens
Location/apartment: 200 tokens
Objects/door_chain + phone: 250 tokens
Relevant memories: 500 tokens
Recent chat: 800 tokens

Total: ~2,600 tokens


Instead of dumping a giant world state.

Tool model for the LLM

Eventually, yes, you could expose tools like:

list_soul_files(soul_id)
read_soul_file(path)
search_soul_vault(query, slots)
get_relationship_file(entity_id)
get_location_file(location_id)
get_object_file(object_id)
propose_soul_patch(target_file, patch_type, evidence)


But I would not let the narrator call those freely mid-response yet.

Better:

context compiler calls tools before prompt
narrator receives selected context
evaluator proposes structured changes after response
code applies validated patches


That keeps the narrator fast and controlled.

Where this fits in the roadmap

This is basically a combination of:

Memory Provenance V1
Memory Inspector V1
.mne Bundle V2
Retrieval Quality V2
Entity Separation Hardening


I would name it:

Soul Vault V1


Progress position:

Current DB/ledger memory system          ███████░░░ 75%
Readable Soul Vault architecture         ██░░░░░░░░ 20%
Memory provenance needed first           ██░░░░░░░░ 20%

Suggested architecture
Soul Vault V1:
DB remains canonical.
Markdown files are generated projections.
User/agent edits go through import/patch validation.


Later:

Soul Vault V2:
Markdown + JSON frontmatter becomes editable source.
DB becomes index/cache.
Patch ledger still records every change.


Do not jump straight to V2.

Minimal implementation path
Step 1, export readable vault

Add command:

export_soul_vault(soul_id)


Creates:

core.md
status.md
relationships/*.md
locations/*.md
objects/*.md
memories/*.md
provenance/*.jsonl


Read-only export at first.

Step 2, use vault as debug inspector

Let Dev Console open these generated files.

Open Soul Vault
Open relationship file
Open location file
Open object file
Open source memory

Step 3, build retrieval from sections

Context compiler can use the same segment model internally:

select_vault_sections_for_turn()

Step 4, controlled imports

Allow editing, but only through:

validate_soul_vault_patch()
apply_soul_vault_patch()


Not raw write.

What I would not do yet
[✗] replace SQLite immediately
[✗] let LLM rewrite core.md directly
[✗] read every markdown file every turn
[✗] use markdown formatting as the only schema
[✗] remove patch ledger
[✗] remove provenance/source addresses

Bottom line

Your idea is good. I would absolutely add it.

But the correct version is:

Soul Vault = segmented, human-readable projection + retrieval surface
DB/ledger = canonical truth and safety system
LLM = patch proposer, not direct file editor
code = validator and applier


That would make Mnemosyne much easier for agents to work on, much easier for users to understand, and much better for long-session memory retrieval.

I would not do it before the next long dogfood, but I would put it right after Memory Provenance / Inspector V1 as:

P1.5 Soul Vault V1

**ChatGPT:**

You are right to push back. My previous answer was too conservative in one direction.

The better answer is:

Yes, a specialized AI should manage Soul files.

No, it should not be an unrestricted narrator randomly rewriting canon.

The right design is:
AI has semantic authority.
Code has structural authority.
Ledger has historical authority.
User has override authority.

1. Why can Claude Code / Hermes / agents rewrite enterprise code if AI is unreliable?

Because they are not trusted in a vacuum.

They work because coding has strong external checks:

AI writes code
→ compiler checks syntax/types
→ tests check behavior
→ linter checks style
→ git diff shows exact change
→ CI rejects broken builds
→ human can review/rollback


So the real model is not:

AI is perfectly reliable, therefore let it write anything.


It is:

AI is powerful but fallible, therefore put it inside a verification loop.


That absolutely applies to Soul files too.

The mistake would be letting the AI rewrite core.md, relationships/default_player.md, and status.md with no diff, no schema, no provenance, no rollback, and no validation. But letting a specialized AI maintain those files through a controlled patch system? That is reasonable.

2. You are right: a specialized Soul Librarian AI makes sense

Asking the narrator to do everything is too much.

The better architecture is multi-agent:

Narrator AI:
writes scene prose only

Evaluator AI:
extracts structured facts from the latest turn

Soul Librarian AI:
maintains readable Soul files and decides where memories belong

Code / Engine:
validates structure, applies patches, indexes files, tracks provenance

User / OOC:
can correct canon, reject memory, or force an override


That is cleaner than forcing rigid code to “understand” story meaning.

The key distinction

Rigid code should not decide:

"Did Aurora's trust increase?"
"Is this memory relationship-relevant?"
"Is this unresolved tension or just body language?"
"Should this belong to location memory or current emotional state?"


That is semantic work. LLMs are better at it.

Rigid code should decide:

Is the patch valid?
Does the target file exist?
Is the source quote real?
Is the memory attached to a turn_id?
Is this update allowed to modify core.md?
Did this patch break the schema?
Can we roll it back?


That is structural work. Code is better at it.

So yes, Soul files can work

The architecture should be:

souls/aurora_schwarz/
├── manifest.json
├── core.md
├── status.md
├── relationships/
│   └── default_player.md
├── locations/
│   └── apartment.md
├── objects/
│   └── door_chain.md
├── plots/
│   ├── current.md
│   └── resolved.md
├── memories/
│   ├── relationship_memory.md
│   ├── unresolved_tension.md
│   ├── recent_emotional_state.md
│   └── world_location_memory.md
└── provenance/
    └── memory_sources.jsonl


But the Soul Librarian should update them like this:

read relevant files
→ read latest turn / evaluator rows
→ propose patch
→ code validates patch
→ code applies atomically
→ ledger records old/new diff
→ retrieval index updates


Not:

LLM opens core.md and freestyle rewrites everything.

What “clear boundaries” should look like
Narrator
Allowed:
- write scene
- portray NPC/Soul behavior
- use selected Soul context

Not allowed:
- edit Soul files
- decide durable memory
- change canon directly

Evaluator
Allowed:
- say what changed in structured form
- provide evidence quotes
- classify event/object/relationship/memory rows

Not allowed:
- directly mutate files
- invent facts not in latest exchange

Soul Librarian
Allowed:
- update core/status/relationship/location/object/memory files
- merge duplicate memories
- move stale plot to resolved
- summarize long memory into shorter durable form

Required:
- cite source turn/message
- produce diff
- mark confidence
- never erase without archive/replace record

Engine Code
Allowed:
- validate patch shape
- enforce file permissions
- apply patch
- update DB/index
- rollback
- reject malformed or unsupported edits

Not required:
- understand literary nuance


This is the team structure you are describing. I agree with it.

The better model is not “less AI control”

It is typed AI control.

Bad:

AI can write any file however it wants.


Good:

AI can call specific tools:
- update_status_section
- append_relationship_memory
- resolve_plot_thread
- update_object_state
- propose_core_identity_change
- invalidate_memory


Each tool has limits.

Example:

update_status_section:
- can modify current emotion, focus, health, equipment
- cannot modify core identity
- expires naturally unless reinforced


Example:

propose_core_identity_change:
- requires high-confidence evidence
- requires multiple turns or user confirmation
- creates review item before applying


That gives you low friction without chaos.

How OOC corrections fit

You are also right that talking OOC to the AI is part of the solution.

If the user says:

OOC: No, Aurora is not scared here. She is cautious but curious.


Then the system should create a correction patch:

correction:
target: recent_emotional_state
old: scared / fear increase
new: cautious curiosity
source: user_ooc_correction
authority: high


Then:

- current status updates
- bad memory is invalidated or corrected
- relationship/fear delta can be reversed or softened
- provenance records user correction


That is much better than pretending the system will never be wrong.

Your intuition is correct on friction

For a creative RP memory app, too much rigid structure kills usability.

You do not want the user constantly doing database surgery. You want:

"That memory is wrong."
"She should remember the jacket."
"That was OOC, not canon."
"Move this to resolved."
"She trusts him less, not more."


Then the Soul Librarian handles the details.

That is the right UX.

My recommended architecture
Canonical layer:
SQLite patch ledger + IDs + provenance

Readable layer:
Soul Vault markdown files

Semantic editor:
Soul Librarian AI

Safety layer:
tool schemas + validation + diff + rollback

Retrieval layer:
indexes sections, not whole files


The markdown files can become the main human/agent interface, while the DB/ledger remains the safety net.

Implementation progression

Do not jump straight to full editable Soul Vault. Build it in levels.

Soul Vault V0:
export readable files only

Soul Vault V1:
Soul Librarian proposes patches, code applies them

Soul Vault V2:
user can edit files, system validates/imports diffs

Soul Vault V3:
retrieval engine uses files as first-class memory sections

Progress impact
Current DB memory system              ███████░░░ 75%
Soul Librarian concept                ███░░░░░░░ 30%
Readable Soul Vault                   ██░░░░░░░░ 20%
Safe editable Soul Vault              █░░░░░░░░░ 10%

Bottom line

You are not wrong.

A specialized AI managing Soul files is probably the right long-term direction for Mnemosyne.

The correction is only this:

Do not trust AI because it is perfect.
Trust AI because it is inside a role, tool, diff, validation, and rollback system.


So yes:

Narrator writes story.
Evaluator extracts facts.
Soul Librarian manages Soul library.
Code enforces rules and applies changes.
User can correct through OOC.


That is a strong architecture.

That is much cheaper than adding a separate Soul Librarian AI pass every turn.

Correct architecture
Narrator AI
→ writes scene

Evaluator AI
→ writes structured context-update script

Engine
→ parses/repairs/validates script
→ applies patch into DB
→ materializes readable Soul files
→ indexes relevant sections for future prompts


So instead of a full second “library manager AI,” the evaluator becomes the semantic agent.

Important correction

Do not execute actual arbitrary code.

Bad:

eval("update Aurora core identity however the AI wants")


Good:

SoulPatch DSL / JSON / TOML-like script


The evaluator writes something code-shaped, but it is not executable code. It is a declarative patch language.

Think:

SET status.emotion = "cautious but softening"
APPEND relationship.default_player.memory = "He stayed outside the threshold and did not pressure her to open the door."
SET object.door_chain.status = "engaged"
ADD unresolved_tension = "Aurora is deciding whether the visitor is safe enough to admit."


Then the engine turns that into file updates.

Why this is better

It solves the exact problem you described:

LLM understands story meaning.
Rigid code enforces file structure.
System repairs malformed output.
Soul files become readable.
No extra full AI pass every turn.


This is basically evaluator_form_v1 evolving into SoulPatch V1.

Example output from evaluator

Instead of only this:

{
  "relationship_rows": [],
  "memory_rows": []
}


Eventually it could output this:

SOULPATCH v1

TARGET soul: aurora_schwarz

UPDATE status.current_emotion:
  value: cautious, guarded, but slightly reassured
  evidence: "her grip on the deadbolt has unconsciously relaxed"

UPDATE relationship.default_player.boundary_pressure:
  direction: decrease
  evidence: "You don't have to open the door"

APPEND relationship.default_player.memory:
  content: The visitor stayed outside the threshold with visible hands and did not pressure Aurora to open the door.
  slot: relationship_memory
  confidence: medium
  evidence: "I stay outside the threshold with my hands visible..."

UPDATE object.door_chain.status:
  value: still engaged, keeping the door only partly open
  evidence: "The chain still holds the door an inch from fully open"


Then code applies it into:

status.md
relationships/default_player.md
objects/door_chain.md
memories/relationship_memory.md

The key is controlled freedom

The evaluator can be expressive, but only inside allowed commands.

Allowed patch commands:

UPDATE_STATUS
APPEND_MEMORY
UPDATE_RELATIONSHIP
UPDATE_OBJECT
UPDATE_LOCATION
OPEN_PLOT
RESOLVE_PLOT
INVALIDATE_MEMORY
ADD_CORRECTION


Not allowed:

DELETE_CORE
REWRITE_ALL
EDIT_RANDOM_FILE
RUN_SCRIPT
REMOVE_PROVENANCE


That gives you low friction while keeping safety.

Best version for your current system

Do not replace evaluator_form_v1 immediately. Add a layer after it.

Current:
evaluator_form_v1 rows
→ EnginePatch
→ DB

Next:
evaluator_form_v1 rows
→ EnginePatch
→ DB
→ materialize Soul files


Then later:

evaluator_form_v1 / SoulPatch hybrid
→ Engine validates
→ DB + Soul files


Do not make files canonical yet. Make them generated projections first.

Suggested files
souls/aurora_schwarz/

Practical roadmap
Soul Files V0:
Generate readable markdown files from current DB state.
No editing yet.

Soul Files V1:
Evaluator patches update DB.
DB materializes files.
Files are readable/debuggable.

Soul Files V2:
Evaluator emits richer SoulPatch commands.

Soul Files V3:
User/OOC corrections create patch commands.
Files update automatically.

Soul Files V4:
Optional file-edit import, with validation and rollback.

Use:

Narrator AI + Evaluator AI + Engine Compiler

**ChatGPT:**

Yes. Exactly.

Same system.
Same evaluator.
Same patch validation.
Same DB/ledger safety.

But instead of materializing Soul state into one giant blob/file,
materialize it into a folder of focused files.

Current model
Narrator response
→ Evaluator form rows
→ EnginePatch
→ DB / ledger / materialized state
→ next prompt context

Proposed model
Narrator response
→ Evaluator form rows
→ EnginePatch
→ DB / ledger
→ Soul folder projection
→ context compiler selects relevant files/sections
→ next prompt context


So yes, not a totally new architecture. More like:

Current system + Soul folder materialization + smarter section retrieval

The folder is not replacing the evaluator

The evaluator still does the semantic work:

relationship changed
object state changed
memory candidate created
unresolved tension appeared
current emotional state changed


The engine still does the hard safety work:

validate row
repair aliases
reject bad rows
apply patch
record provenance
rollback if needed


The folder just becomes the readable and retrievable shape:

souls/aurora_schwarz/
├── core.md
├── status.md
├── relationships/
│   └── default_player.md
├── locations/
│   └── apartment.md
├── objects/
│   ├── door_chain.md
│   └── phone.md
├── plots/
│   ├── current.md
│   └── resolved.md
├── memories/
│   ├── relationship_memory.md
│   ├── unresolved_tension.md
│   ├── recent_emotional_state.md
│   └── world_location_memory.md
└── provenance/
    └── sources.jsonl

Why folder beats one file

One big file creates the same problem as one giant commands.rs:

too much unrelated context
hard to inspect
hard to patch safely
hard to retrieve selectively
easy to accidentally rewrite too much


A folder lets the system do:

Need relationship with player?
→ read relationships/default_player.md

Need apartment continuity?
→ read locations/apartment.md

Need door chain state?
→ read objects/door_chain.md

Need current emotional state?
→ read status.md

Need durable identity?
→ read compressed core.md


That is much more token-efficient.

Best implementation model

Do it in phases.

Soul Folder V0:
DB remains source of truth.
Generate folder files from DB for inspection/export only.

Soul Folder V1:
Context compiler can read selected generated files/sections.
Still no direct file editing.

Soul Folder V2:
Evaluator patches update DB, then DB regenerates affected files.
Files become the human/agent-readable surface.

Soul Folder V3:
Allow controlled user/agent file edits, but only through validate/import patch tools.

What not to do

Do not do this:

Evaluator AI directly rewrites random markdown files freely.
Narrator reads the whole folder every turn.
Markdown becomes the only source of truth immediately.


That creates corruption and token waste.

What to do

Do this:

Evaluator AI writes structured patch rows.
Engine applies them.
Engine materializes folder files.
Context compiler retrieves only relevant files.


So the Soul folder is like a compiled projection of the DB/ledger, not a chaotic free-write scratchpad.

Concrete example

Evaluator produces:

relationship: boundary_pressure increase
evidence: chain still holds the door partly open

memory: unresolved_tension
content: Aurora is deciding whether the visitor is safe enough to admit.

object: door_chain
status: still engaged


Engine writes:

relationships/default_player.md
objects/door_chain.md
memories/unresolved_tension.md
status.md


Next prompt only loads the relevant subset.

Bottom line

Yes:

Our exact current system,
but materialized into a folder of focused Soul files,
with DB/ledger still protecting truth,
and context retrieval selecting only relevant files.


That is the practical version. It keeps the current evaluator architecture, avoids adding another real-time AI pass, and gives you cleaner agent/human inspection plus lower token pressure.

llm-payload-history-1780751388.md	9,718 lines, 182 headers	# Mnemosyne LLM Payload History → Payloads 1–14 → repeated evaluator traces/export	Yes
Answer

It is both, but not equally.

Updater AI quality problem:       ██████░░░░ 60%
System inference/acceptance gap:  ███████░░░ 70%
System totally failing to apply:  ██░░░░░░░░ 20%


The important distinction:

The updater AI is noticing the right relationship-relevant moments.
The system is often failing to convert some of those moments into deltas because direction is missing or too ambiguous.


So it is not simply “the updater AI is trash.” It is also not “the system ignores all feedback.” The live logs prove the system does apply relationship patches when the row becomes valid.

What the logs prove
1. The updater AI is finding relationship-relevant events

In the tiny door test, the visible scene gave strong boundary/trust evidence: Aurora keeps the chain engaged, the visitor stays outside, hands visible, and says she does not have to open the door. 

mnemosyne-local-mock-efdf8055-f…

That is good source material.

In the longer run, the evaluator also produced relationship rows for conflict and boundary pressure, meaning it recognized that those were relationship-relevant. The rejected rows were not random garbage, they were semantically plausible rows:

conflict:
"strange, sharp thrill of being cornered"

boundary_pressure:
"Showing up with my manager's number"


Both were rejected because direction stayed null. 

mnemosyne-local-mock-438c34ce-7…

2. The system can take relationship feedback when the row is valid

There are successful rows too. One run shows:

form_rows_submitted: 9
form_rows_accepted: 9
form_rows_rejected: 0
relationship_delta_created


So the engine is not globally failing to ingest relationship feedback. 

mnemosyne-local-mock-1bbda14a-0…

In the newer longer run, an intimacy row was accepted and compiled into a relationship delta, while the boundary_pressure row beside it was rejected because direction was missing. That is the clearest proof: same evaluator response, same system, one relationship row accepted, one rejected. 

mnemosyne-local-mock-438c34ce-7…

So what exactly is failing?

The failure is here:

relationship row exists
dimension exists
evidence exists
source/target can be inferred
BUT direction is null
→ system cannot confidently map it to + or -
→ validation rejects with direction_missing_uncertain


That means the problem is not numeric score calculation first.

The updater usually is not saying:

boundary_pressure = +2
comfort = -1
trust = +1


It is saying something more like:

dimension: boundary_pressure
evidence: "hands visible... not threatening"
direction: null


Then the code has to infer whether that means:

boundary_pressure increase?
boundary_pressure decrease?
comfort increase?
trust increase?


When the phrase table does not know the evidence pattern, it rejects.

Most accurate diagnosis
Primary bug class:
relationship direction under-specification

Secondary bug class:
system phrase-table too narrow

Not the main bug:
relationship patch application totally broken


The updater AI is being “bad” in the sense that it often omits the most important field: direction.

The system is being “bad” in the sense that it does not yet infer enough obvious missing directions from evidence like:

hands visible
not threatening
stays outside threshold
uncurls fingers
shoulders drop
cornered
manager number
deadline pressure

What to fix

Do not overhaul the updater. Do one narrow rule patch:

Relationship Direction Inference V2

Add:
1. boundary_pressure decrease
   - hands visible
   - not threatening
   - stays outside threshold
   - does not pressure her to open
   - patient stillness

2. comfort increase
   - fingers uncurl
   - shoulders drop
   - grip relaxes
   - posture softens
   - can process that tone

3. conflict increase
   - cornered
   - pressure
   - deadline pressure
   - manager number used as leverage

4. boundary_pressure increase
   - showing up with manager's number
   - pressure about overdue work
   - leverage
   - forced confrontation


Also add one prompt nudge to the evaluator later, but code-side inference is more reliable than begging the LLM to always fill the form perfectly.

Bottom line
It is not “AI bad OR system bad.”
It is “AI gives semantically useful but incomplete relationship rows, and the system is still too conservative/narrow at converting incomplete rows.”


The right fix is not replacing the updater AI.

The right fix is:

keep the evaluator
tighten the evaluator prompt slightly
expand deterministic direction inference
keep rejecting truly ambiguous rows
